HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Thursday, November 26, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Computational Study Identifies Soy Isoflavone Genistein as Dengue Envelope Inhibitor by November 23, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 Computational Study Identifies Soy Isoflavone Genistein as Dengue Envelope Inhibitor Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram In a computational investigation published in In Silico Research in Biomedicine, researchers evaluated dozens of natural phytochemicals to identify potential inhibitors capable of targeting the structural envelope protein of the dengue virus across multiple viral lineages. Dengue virus, an enveloped pathogen belonging to the Flaviviridae family, is transmitted to humans through the bites of infected female Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus mosquitoes. Globally, dengue represents an escalating public health crisis across tropical and subtropical territories, including South-east Asia, Latin America, the Pacific Islands, and the Caribbean. Epidemiological assessments recorded by the World Health Organization report that over 390 million people contract dengue annually, resulting in roughly 22,000 fatal outcomes, with the Asian continent bearing approximately 67 percent of all clinical cases. Despite the clinical spectrum ranging from sudden high fever, retro-orbital eye pain, arthralgia, headache, and severe myalgia to life-threatening conditions such as dengue hemorrhagic fever and dengue shock syndrome, no targeted antiviral pharmaceuticals are currently approved for clinical therapy. Supportive treatment relies almost entirely on careful fluid balance management to mitigate severe dehydration. To overcome the current therapeutic deficit, investigators have increasingly focused on plant-derived secondary metabolites that display broad-spectrum antimicrobial, anti-inflammatory, antioxidant, and antiviral characteristics. The structural architecture of the dengue virion features an approximately eleven-kilobase single-stranded positive-sense RNA genome that encodes three structural proteins comprising the capsid, membrane, and envelope proteins, along with seven non-structural proteins designated NS1, NS2A, NS2B, NS3, NS4A, NS4B, and NS5. Among these, the surface-exposed envelope glycoprotein plays an indispensable role in mediating infection by facilitating virion attachment to host cell surface receptors and driving subsequent membrane fusion. Structurally, the envelope monomer is organized into three distinct structural units: domain I, which acts as a central hinge orchestrating conformational shifts; domain II, which contains a highly conserved hydrophobic fusion loop responsible for dimerization and host membrane insertion; and domain III, which functions as the primary receptor-binding domain. Because the envelope protein also mediates viral hemagglutination and neutralizes host immunological defenses, disrupting its structural integrity represents a strategic approach for halting infection. Discover more Read Med Journals Try Artisan Pasta Health The research team devised an integrated in silico screening pipeline that began with the systematic collection of dengue virus envelope protein sequences deposited in the UniProt database across four antigenically distinct serotypes, designated DENV-1, DENV-2, DENV-3, and DENV-4. Their sequence retrieval yielded eighteen distinct strains for DENV-1, eleven for DENV-2, five for DENV-3, and eight for DENV-4, representing viral isolates collected over multiple decades from Asia, South America, North America, and Australia. The polypeptide chains displayed an average length of 488 plus or minus 8.09 amino acid residues. To pinpoint structurally conserved target domains across serotypes, the investigators conducted multiple sequence alignment using the ClustalW program within the Clustal Omega suite. Based on previous structural literature, six specific functional pockets and active site ranges were mapped along the envelope sequence: amino acid residues 45 to 65, 110 to 140, 190 to 225, 255 to 290, 300 to 345, and 346 to 390, confirming homologous binding regions shared among diverse geographic variants. Discover more Study Biology Study Chemistry Order Vitamins Check Symptoms Three-dimensional structural models of these peptide segments were subsequently generated using the SWISS-MODEL homology modeling server through BLAST and HHblits template selection algorithms. Model reliability was confirmed through rigorous structural validation criteria, requiring favorable MolProbity scores, Ramachandran plot favored percentages of 98 percent or greater, sequence identities exceeding 40 percent, and QMEAN Z-scores higher than negative 4.0. To eliminate structural redundancy, root-mean-square distance calculations were performed in PyMOL software to determine structural convergence among predicted peptide conformations. This structural filtering selected six representative viral sequences: strain A0A0D4D8J0 for segment 45 to 65, strain A0A0N7H514 for segment 110 to 140, strain A0A0D4D8J0 for segments 190 to 225 and 300 to 345, strain A0A0D4D9E3 for segment 255 to 290, and strain J9WMM0 for segment 346 to 390. Active binding crevices within these peptide models were mapped computationally using the CASTp 3.0 server configured with a default radius probe of 1.4 angstroms. Parallel to the viral target preparation, the researchers compiled a curated library of seventy-one plant-derived bioactive antiviral compounds from peer-reviewed literature, retrieving their canonical SMILES notations and three-dimensional spatial structures from the PubChem chemical database. The entire library underwent rigorous virtual ADMET screening via the ADMETlab 2.0 platform to evaluate human intestinal absorption, cellular permeability, metabolic clearance, half-life parameters, blood-brain barrier penetration, and potential organ toxicities. Drug-likeness was evaluated against Lipinski’s rule of five, which specifies a molecular weight below 500 grams per mole, fewer than three hydrogen-bond donors, fewer than twelve hydrogen-bond acceptors, a octanol-water partition coefficient log P below five, and fewer than twelve rotatable bonds. Four candidate molecules successfully satisfied Lipinski rules, the GlaxoSmithKline criteria, and the Golden Triangle rule: the flavone baicalein, the flavonol quercetin, the sesquiterpene lactone artecanin, and the isoflavone genistein. All four demonstrated favorable Caco-2 cell permeability values exceeding negative 5.15, acceptable human hepatotoxicity and skin sensitization profiles, low mutagenic AMES probabilities, and satisfactory drug clearance rates. Rigid molecular docking simulations were executed using the AutoDock 1.5.6 software package across each of the six designated envelope functional pockets, calculating binding free energy values based on classical thermodynamic principles. A uniform grid box measuring 60 by 60 by 60 grid points was constructed in AutoGrid to calculate electrostatic, van der Waals, and solvation energy grids. Across all evaluated active pockets, the four shortlisted phytochemicals displayed pronounced docking affinities, yet genistein consistently registered the strongest overall binding scores. For pocket 45 to 65, baicalein and quercetin both docked with negative 4.6 kilocalories per mole, artecanin with negative 4.4 kilocalories per mole, and genistein with negative 4.9 kilocalories per mole. In the 110 to 140 pocket, baicalein recorded negative 4.7 kilocalories per mole, while quercetin, artecanin, and genistein achieved negative 5.0 kilocalories per mole. In the 190 to 225 pocket, binding values were negative 6.6 kilocalories per mole for baicalein, negative 7.0 for quercetin, negative 6.4 for artecanin, and negative 7.1 for genistein. Docking continued across the remaining three envelope regions, showing consistent intermolecular affinity. In pocket 255 to 290, baicalein scored negative 5.7 kilocalories per mole, artecanin negative 5.6 kilocalories per mole, and both quercetin and genistein negative 5.9 kilocalories per mole. In pocket 300 to 345, baicalein and genistein reached negative 6.0 kilocalories per mole, while quercetin and artecanin reached negative 5.9 kilocalories per mole. Most notably, within the 346 to 390 pocket, genistein established the strongest overall interaction, reaching a binding energy of negative 7.6 kilocalories per mole, outperforming baicalein at negative 7.1 kilocalories per mole, quercetin at negative 7.0 kilocalories per mole, and artecanin at negative 6.8 kilocalories per mole. Across all tested envelope sites, genistein displayed an average binding affinity of negative 6.06 plus or minus 1.1 kilocalories per mole. Within the high-affinity 346 to 390 site of strain J9WMM0, molecular visualization using Discovery Studio revealed an intricate interactome comprising seven van der Waals contacts with threonine 1, leucine 6, proline 26, glutamic acid 30, serine 31, tyrosine 32, and isoleucine 33, alongside a vital hydrogen bond with glutamine 2, and three pi-alkyl linkages involving arginine 5, alanine 24, and proline 27. To investigate whether this top-performing complex could preserve structural stability in a realistic aqueous environment, the researchers conducted 100-nanosecond molecular dynamics simulations using the GROMACS package under the CHARMM27 force field. The genistein-peptide complex was solvated in an explicit TIP3P water cubic box measuring 90.0 by 90.0 by 90.0 nanometers, neutralized with sodium and chloride counter-ions, and energy-minimized to eliminate steric clashes. Simulations ran at a regulated temperature of 300 Kelvin and atmospheric pressure of 1 atmosphere, utilizing the Nose-Hoover thermostat, the Martyna-Tobias-Klein barostat, and particle mesh Ewald electrostatics with a grid spacing of 0.8 angstroms. Trajectory coordinates were logged every 1000 picoseconds, allowing detailed measurement of root-mean-square deviation, root-mean-square fluctuation, radius of gyration, solvent-accessible surface area, and intermolecular hydrogen bonding throughout the 100-nanosecond duration. Analysis of the simulation trajectories corroborated the structural integrity and conformational stability of the genistein-envelope complex. Root-mean-square deviation of the complex equilibrated during the initial 10 nanoseconds and remained stable between 0.7 and 0.8 nanometers from 20 to 60 nanoseconds. Although a minor elevation toward 1.6 nanometers appeared after 80 nanoseconds, the trajectory re-stabilized near 1.1 nanometers toward the simulation endpoint, confirming persistent ligand containment without displacement from the active pocket. Residue-level root-mean-square fluctuation plots revealed that while terminal peptide ends exhibited expected flexibility near 0.8 nanometers, core interacting residues displayed restrained fluctuations between 0.2 and 0.4 nanometers. Furthermore, the radius of gyration maintained a narrow range of 1.03 to 1.27 nanometers, demonstrating sustained structural compactness. Solvent-accessible surface area values fluctuated steadily between 38 and 48 square nanometers, indicating reliable solvent exposure and stable peptide folding. Intermolecular hydrogen bonding remained resilient, typically exhibiting one to three continuous hydrogen bonds and intermittently reaching four to five contacts, while principal component analysis confirmed tightly clustered conformational dynamics. Thermodynamic stability was further quantified through Molecular Mechanics/Generalized Born Surface Area calculations using the GMX_MMPBSA tool, which revealed a favorable net binding free energy of approximately negative 18 kilocalories per mole. Gas-phase molecular mechanics contributions totaled roughly negative 35 kilocalories per mole, dominated by favorable van der Waals interactions of approximately negative 25 kilocalories per mole and electrostatic interactions contributing negative 10 kilocalories per mole, illustrating strong hydrophobic packing. Although the total polar solvation energy presented an unfavorable barrier of approximately positive 20 kilocalories per mole, it was partly compensated by favorable non-polar solvation of negative 3 kilocalories per mole, ensuring an overall spontaneous binding state. While genistein occurs widely in dietary legumes, particularly mature soybeans yielding between 5.6 and 276 milligrams per 100 grams, the study authors emphasize that their computational findings are strictly dry-lab predictions. Wet-lab experimental investigations, including in vitro viral plaque reduction neutralization assays and subsequent in vivo trials, remain essential before any clinical antiviral therapeutic claims can be established. 阻断 XPO1 核输出促进 登革病毒 在培养细胞中的复制 - Bioengineer.org

Blocking XPO1 Nuclear Export Promotes Dengue Virus Replication in Cultured Cells - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报这项研究于 2026 年 11 月 24 日发表,探讨了 XPO1 核输出途径在 登革病毒 复制中的作用。研究人员使用 Huh-7 人肝癌细胞和新生 CD-1 乳鼠大脑来研究阻断 XPO1 如何影响 登革病毒 非结构蛋白 3 (NS3) 的运输及其对线粒体动力学和先天 抗病毒药物 防御的下游影响。研究结果表明,抑制 XPO1 会导致 NS3 在细胞核和线粒体中积累,将线粒体结构重组为拉长的网络,并减少 I 型干扰素的分泌,从而促进 登革病毒 的复制。相比之下,在类似条件下,寨卡病毒 NS3 不会在线粒体中积聚。该研究强调了核细胞质运输在病毒复制中的重要性,并表明针对 XPO1 可能是 抗病毒药物 干预措施的潜在策略。然而,该研究仅限于体外实验,并未提供有关 登革病毒 暴发 或公共卫生影响的临床或流行病学数据。

时间
该研究发表于2026年11月24日。实验使用感染登革病毒 2型或寨卡病毒的Huh-7细胞进行,感染复数为5。分析的时间点是感染后十二小时和二十四小时。
地点与对象
该研究是使用培养的人类 Huh-7 细胞和新生 CD-1 乳鼠大脑进行的。该研究涉及登革病毒 2型和寨卡病毒,重点关注登革病毒作为目标实体。
事件
研究人员研究了阻断 XPO1 核输出途径如何影响 登革病毒 在培养的人肝癌细胞中的复制。他们发现抑制 XPO1 通过改变 NS3 的亚细胞分布和线粒体动力学来促进 登革病毒 复制。
规模、影响与风险
研究表明,感染后 12 小时内,17.5% 的线粒体呈 NS3 金标记阳性,24 小时后增加至 27.5%。研究结果表明体外存在亲病毒环境,但所提供的证据中没有报告具体的病例数、死亡数或风险水平。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是使用培养细胞和动物组织在体外进行的。它涉及 XPO1 小分子抑制剂的使用和各种分析技术。该研究被归类为科学研究,其结果基于实验数据而不是临床或流行病学结果。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Blocking XPO1 Nuclear Export Promotes Dengue Virus Replication in Cultured Cells - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
This study, published on November 24, 2026, explores the role of the XPO1 nuclear export pathway in dengue virus replication. Researchers used Huh-7 human hepatoma cells and neonatal CD-1 suckling mouse brains to investigate how blocking XPO1 affects the trafficking of dengue virus non-structural protein 3 (NS3) and its downstream effects on mitochondrial dynamics and innate antiviral defenses. The findings reveal that inhibiting XPO1 promotes dengue virus replication by causing NS3 to accumulate in both the nucleus and mitochondria, reorganizing mitochondrial architecture into elongated networks, and reducing type I interferon secretion. In contrast, Zika virus NS3 did not accumulate in mitochondria under similar conditions. The study highlights the importance of nucleocytoplasmic transport in viral replication and suggests that targeting XPO1 could be a potential strategy for antiviral interventions. However, the research is limited to in vitro experiments and does not provide clinical or epidemiological data on dengue virus outbreaks or public health impacts.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Thursday, November 26, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Biology Blocking XPO1 Nuclear Export Promotes Dengue Virus Replication in Cultured Cells by November 24, 2026 in Biology Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 Blocking XPO1 Nuclear Export Promotes Dengue Virus Replication in Cultured Cells Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Inhibiting exportin 1, a fundamental cellular transport receptor responsible for carrying proteins and ribonucleoprotein complexes out of the nucleus, unexpectedly enhances the replication of dengue virus serotype 2 in human hepatoma cell cultures. In an investigation conducted using Huh-7 human hepatocellular carcinoma cells, researchers evaluated how blocking the exportin 1, or XPO1, nuclear export pathway affects the trafficking of the viral non-structural protein 3, known as NS3, alongside its downstream consequences on mitochondrial dynamics and innate antiviral defenses. Although orthoflavivirus replication cycles operate predominantly within the host cytoplasm in association with membranes derived from the endoplasmic reticulum, viral factors routinely transit into the cellular nucleus. Rather than restricting viral propagation, targeted pharmacological disruption of XPO1-mediated export drove the accumulation of NS3 in both nuclear and mitochondrial compartments, reorganized mitochondrial architecture into elongated networks, and blunted type I interferon secretion, establishing a distinctly pro-viral environment in vitro. The study specifically addressed how nucleocytoplasmic transport mechanics influence the intracellular sorting of dengue virus non-structural protein 3, a multifunctional enzyme possessing essential viral protease and helicase activities required for polyprotein processing and RNA replication. Previous investigations had established that dengue virus proteins, notably the polymerase NS5 and the protease-helicase NS3, utilize classical importin pathways to access the nucleus during the initial phases of infection, where they degrade structural nucleoporins within the nuclear pore complex and interfere with host transcriptional networks. While earlier strategies aimed at blocking nuclear import with compounds such as ivermectin succeeded in restricting viral replication, the precise role played by nuclear export pathways and their structural link to extra-nuclear organelles had remained largely uncharacterized. The research team explored whether obstructing nuclear export would cause dengue virus NS3 to redistribute abnormally between the cell nucleus and the mitochondrial network, and whether this trafficking bottleneck altered organellar integrity and cellular immune competence. Discover more Study Anatomy Find Study Aids Read Med Journals To evaluate these transport mechanisms, the scientists propagated dengue virus type 2, using the New Guinea C reference strain, alongside a comparative Zika virus strain, using neonatal CD-1 suckling mouse brains under approved institutional animal care protocols. These neonatal murine brain tissues served strictly as an in vivo viral propagation substrate rather than an animal disease model, yielding clarified viral stocks for downstream cell culture experiments. Cultured human Huh-7 cells, maintained in high-glucose Advanced Dulbecco’s Modified Eagle Medium supplemented with fetal bovine serum and antibiotics, were infected with dengue virus type 2 or Zika virus at a multiplicity of infection of five. Alongside authentic infections, the authors also introduced plasmid constructs encoding dengue virus NS2B-NS3 into uninfected Huh-7 cells through electroporation, permitting the examination of NS3 sorting and mitochondrial alterations independent of the full viral replication cycle or secondary structural proteins. Discover more Order DNA Kits Study Biology Study Chemistry Check Symptoms Using transmission immunoelectron microscopy with colloidal gold-labeled antibodies, the investigators systematically mapped the subcellular locations of NS3 in Huh-7 cells at twelve and twenty-four hours post-infection. In cells infected with dengue virus type 2, colloidal gold particles indicating NS3 were clearly resolved not only throughout the cytoplasm and the nucleus but also directly within the interior of mitochondria. Quantitative assessment revealed that 17.5 percent of mitochondria were positive for NS3 gold labeling at twelve hours post-infection, an abundance that expanded to 27.5 percent of mitochondria by twenty-four hours. In sharp contrast, parallel experiments performed in Zika virus-infected Huh-7 cells showed that zero percent of mitochondria contained NS3 gold particles at either time point, even though Zika virus NS3 readily accumulated within the cytoplasm and nucleus. Multiple sequence alignment of orthoflavivirus proteins demonstrated that a defined mitochondrial targeting sequence, previously reported in dengue virus serotype 1 NS3, is preserved across dengue serotypes but lacks sufficient sequence conservation in Zika virus NS3, explaining the striking disparity in organellar tropism between the two viruses. To probe the dependence of NS3 distribution on cellular export mechanisms, the researchers employed two distinct small-molecule inhibitors of XPO1: leptomycin B, an irreversible covalent inhibitor binding cysteine 528 of the transport receptor, and selinexor, a reversible first-generation selective inhibitor of nuclear export developed for clinical oncology. Treatment of Huh-7 cells with 37 nanomolar leptomycin B for twenty-four hours was first validated using indirect immunofluorescence confocal microscopy and subcellular fractionation assays targeting cyclin B1, an endogenous cellular cargo that relies strictly on XPO1 for nuclear clearance. Whereas cyclin B1 remained predominantly cytoplasmic in vehicle-treated controls, leptomycin B caused marked nuclear retention. Western blot analysis of isolated nuclear and cytoplasmic fractions demonstrated that XPO1 inhibition produced a substantial increase of dengue virus NS3 inside the nucleus. Intriguingly, western blots of purified mitochondrial fractions, validated for purity using the mitochondrial marker TOMM22 alongside nuclear and cytoplasmic controls, demonstrated that leptomycin B treatment concurrently elevated NS3 levels within mitochondria, a finding corroborated in electroporated cells expressing recombinant NS2B3. The scientists next determined how pharmacological export blockade altered mitochondrial ultrastructure. Transmission electron microscopy revealed that uninfected, vehicle-treated Huh-7 cells possessed predominantly small, rounded, or short mitochondria. Following twenty-four hours of exposure to either 37 nanomolar leptomycin B or 200 nanomolar selinexor, uninfected cells exhibited pronounced mitochondrial elongation, reflected by statistically significant increases in total mitochondrial cross-sectional area. Two-dimensional morphometric analysis of confocal images stained for TOMM22 confirmed this remodeling, showing substantial rises in mitochondrial surface area, perimeter length, and network branching index following XPO1 inhibition. When Huh-7 cells were infected with dengue virus type 2 in the absence of inhibitors, mitochondria likewise underwent marked elongation compared to uninfected controls, matching previous observations of virus-induced organelle reshaping. While treating infected cells with leptomycin B or selinexor induced thinner mitochondrial profiles and some structural cristae disorganization, the inhibitors did not amplify the overall elongation beyond the pronounced morphological transformation already driven by dengue infection itself. Because mitochondrial remodeling frequently coincides with changes in organelle turnover and mitochondrial biogenesis, the authors examined mitochondrial mass and biogenesis signaling pathways following XPO1 inhibition. Flow cytometry measurements of Huh-7 cells stained for the outer mitochondrial membrane marker TOMM22 showed that while roughly 60 to 70 percent of cells remained positive across all groups, mean fluorescence intensity rose significantly in uninfected cells treated with leptomycin B or selinexor, indicating an increase in overall mitochondrial mass at the population level. Flow cytometry also revealed that the frequency of dengue-infected cells, identified by intracellular NS3 positivity at twenty-four hours post-infection, expanded from 15 percent in vehicle-treated controls to 26 percent following leptomycin B exposure. Total cellular levels of NS3 protein rose in parallel on western blots. When investigating peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor gamma coactivator 1-alpha, or PGC1-alpha, the master transcriptional regulator of mitochondrial biogenesis, researchers detected a modest, non-significant trend toward elevated protein abundance in treated cells and a transient increase in PGC1-alpha messenger RNA in mock-infected cultures, suggesting that biogenesis pathways may be modestly engaged but remain preliminary under these experimental settings. The downstream consequences of nuclear export blockade directly favored viral proliferation rather than antiviral defense. Transmission electron microscopy of dengue virus-infected Huh-7 cells treated with leptomycin B or selinexor revealed a pronounced proliferation of virus-induced cytoplasmic replication vesicles compared to untreated infected cells. Plaque-forming unit assays conducted on cell culture supernatants showed that both leptomycin B and selinexor significantly increased the output of infectious dengue virus type 2 virions. Quantitative reverse-transcription polymerase chain reaction assays measuring intracellular viral genomes demonstrated an approximate two-fold increase in viral RNA copies following leptomycin B exposure and a six-fold increase in cells treated with selinexor. Notably, this pro-viral enhancement was entirely specific to dengue virus; parallel viral yield assays and focus-forming unit titrations carried out in Zika virus-infected Huh-7 cells showed no significant alterations in infectious particle release or viral RNA accumulation after XPO1 inhibition. To resolve how blocking nuclear export accelerated dengue replication despite triggering cellular stress, the investigators examined the modulation of the type I interferon signaling cascade. Unstimulated Huh-7 cells exhibited minimal baseline interferon activation, prompting the team to pre-stimulate cells with twenty micrograms per milliliter of recombinant human interferon-beta for six hours before virus inoculation. Pre-stimulated cells were subsequently infected with dengue virus type 2 and treated with vehicle, leptomycin B, or selinexor for twenty-four hours. Enzyme-linked immunosorbent assays performed on cell culture supernatants revealed that pre-stimulated, infected cells treated with either nuclear export inhibitor suffered an approximate 50 percent decrease in secreted interferon-beta protein compared to untreated stimulated controls. Paradoxically, quantitative reverse-transcription PCR demonstrated a two- to four-fold elevation in interferon-beta and interferon-stimulated gene IFI44L transcripts across treated cells, indicating that while transcription was initiated, post-transcriptional stages such as mRNA nuclear export, protein translation, or cytokine secretion were severely disrupted by XPO1 inhibition. This sharp reduction in functional interferon-beta secretion directly uncoupled the host cell’s antiviral defense from viral control. In untreated Huh-7 cells, interferon-beta pre-stimulation successfully suppressed dengue virus type 2 particle release into the culture medium. However, when cells were treated with leptomycin B or selinexor, infectious viral titers increased significantly even in the presence of prior interferon priming, overcoming the established antiviral state. The authors contextualize these results by noting that while selinexor promotes proteasomal degradation of XPO1 and leptomycin B covalently modifies the receptor without reducing total XPO1 abundance, both agents elicited matching increases in viral titer, NS3 organellar redistribution, and suppression of cytokine output. The researchers caution that these findings are derived from in vitro human hepatoma cell cultures and neonatal mouse brain propagation stocks, meaning clinical extrapolations regarding selective nuclear export inhibitors in human dengue pathology must account for complex tissue-specific immune responses and differences in viral replication kinetics across distinct host systems. Subject of Research: Role of XPO1-mediated nuclear export in dengue virus type 2 replication and innate immune suppression Article Title: XPO1-dependent nuclear export regulates NS3 localization and promotes DENV-2 replication through mitochondrial remodeling and interferon suppression Article References: Palacios-Rápalo, S. Time
The study was published on November 24, 2026. The experiments were conducted with Huh-7 cells infected with dengue virus type 2 or Zika virus at a multiplicity of infection of five. The time points for analysis were twelve and twenty-four hours post-infection.
Location and population
The research was conducted using cultured human Huh-7 cells and neonatal CD-1 suckling mouse brains. The study involved dengue virus type 2 and Zika virus, with the focus on dengue virus as the target entity.
Event
Researchers investigated how blocking the XPO1 nuclear export pathway affects dengue virus replication in cultured human hepatoma cells. They found that inhibiting XPO1 promotes dengue virus replication by altering the subcellular distribution of NS3 and mitochondrial dynamics.
Scale, impact and risk
The study demonstrated that 17.5% of mitochondria were positive for NS3 gold labeling at twelve hours post-infection, increasing to 27.5% by twenty-four hours. The findings indicate a pro-viral environment in vitro, but no specific case counts, death counts, or risk levels were reported in the supplied evidence.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study was conducted in vitro using cultured cells and animal tissues. It involved the use of small-molecule inhibitors of XPO1 and various analytical techniques. The research is classified as a scientific investigation, and the results are based on experimental data rather than clinical or epidemiological outcomes.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

New dengue cases appear to slow across Tampa Bay: See latest county-by-county numbers

News Brief
Florida health officials report a decrease in locally acquired dengue cases, with 669 confirmed cases in 2026, mostly in Hillsborough and Pinellas Counties. The Tampa Bay area, accounting for 94% of infections, shows a recent decline. One death was reported, and a state of emergency is in place in Hillsborough County. Prevention efforts focus on mosquito control, with no specific treatment available for dengue fever.
Original source text (English)
The Florida Department of Health has released the latest numbers on confirmed cases of dengue fever, a mosquito-borne illness transmitted by the bite of an Aedes mosquito, and it looks like some relief may finally be on the way. According to the latest data, there have been 669 cases of locally acquired dengue fever in Florida in 2026, with about a majority of those cases reported in Hillsborough County . Health officials say new locally acquired dengue cases have been decreasing in recent weeks, but emphasize there is a delay between when symptoms begin and when a case is reported. The Tampa Bay area leads the state in dengue fever cases, accounting for about 94% of infections reported statewide, with the largest increases in Pinellas and Hillsborough Counties. Florida confirmed one death related to the mosquito-borne illness in Hillsborough on Sept. 18. Hillsborough has extended its state of emergency through Nov. 26. A state of emergency gives officials "the ability to quickly take certain actions to ensure the health, safety, and welfare of the community, and provides a path for federal reimbursement of certain expenses." Here's a county-by-county breakdown of dengue cases across Florida. Though no locally acquired dengue fever cases have been reported in Hernando, county health officials have issued a mosquito-borne illness advisory after finding a locally acquired West Nile virus infection in sentinel chickens. As for dengue cases acquired through international travel, Florida health officials say there have been 476 cases in 2026, with 25 reported in just the last week. Tampa Bay area counties reporting international travel-associated dengue cases include Hillsborough (29), Pinellas and Polk (4), Manatee (3), Sarasota (2), and Hernando and Citrus (1). Dengue fever is a viral illness spread by infected mosquitoes to people. According to the World Health Organization (WHO), most people who get dengue do not have symptoms. Those who do may experience high fever, headache, body aches, nausea and rash. Health officials say most people get better in 1-2 weeks, while some may develop more severe symptoms that can lead to hospitalization or death. Other than pain management, there is no specific treatment for dengue. Health leaders say the best way to prevent dengue infection is to avoid mosquito bites. You can do that by wearing insect repellent, wearing protective clothing and draining standing water around your home. Check out our guide on how to prevent dengue infection. What to know about dengue fever as Florida reports 1st death and cases rise - ABC13 Houston

What to know about dengue fever as Florida reports 1st death and cases rise - ABC13 Houston
新闻简报Florida has reported a significant increase in dengue cases, with 152 locally acquired cases confirmed by the week ending Sept. 12, 2026. This marks the highest number since 2023, and the state has confirmed its first dengue-related death of the year in Hillsborough County. The Tampa Bay Times first reported the death as an 80-year-old Tampa woman who succumbed to the illness. The Florida Department of Health has completed its epidemiological investigation but has not disclosed details about the deceased patient, citing privacy concerns. Dengue, a mosquito-borne illness, can lead to severe complications in about one in 20 infected individuals, including shock, internal bleeding, and death. The CDC advises against using aspirin or ibuprofen for dengue patients due to the risk of inflammation or bleeding. Prevention measures include using effective mosquito repellents, wearing long-sleeved clothing, and eliminating stagnant water sources. No specific treatment exists for dengue, and the CDC recommends rest, fluids, and over-the-counter medications to manage symptoms. The FDOH has not provided further details on the case, and the investigation remains ongoing.
查看新闻五要素
时间
随着蚊媒疾病病例持续上升,佛罗里达州卫生部确认了该州今年首例 登革热 死亡病例。希尔斯伯勒县报告了首例死亡病例,截至 9 月 17 日的一周,本地感染病例数达到 152 例。
地点与对象
佛罗里达州,特别是希尔斯伯勒县(包括坦帕),今年报告的登革热病例最多。其他受影响的县包括迈阿密戴德县、奥兰治县、棕榈滩县、帕斯科县和皮内拉斯县。受影响的人群包括通过蚊子叮咬感染登革热的人,但没有提供有关已故患者的具体细节。
事件
佛罗里达州今年已确认 152 例本地感染登革热病例,希尔斯伯勒县报告首例死亡病例。佛罗里达州卫生部证实了这起死亡事件,《坦帕湾时报》首先报道称,一名 80 岁的坦帕妇女于 9 月 17 日死亡。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年9月12周结束,佛罗里达州已确认152例本地感染的登革热病例,这是自2023年以来的最高数字。希尔斯伯勒县报告的病例最多,共134例。登革热可能在约20名感染个体中导致严重疾病,可能引发休克、内出血或死亡。CDC警告不要使用阿司匹林或布洛芬,因为它们可能引发炎症或出血。
应对、状态与不确定性
佛罗里达州卫生部已完成流行病学调查,并承认希尔斯伯勒县出现首例登革热相关死亡病例。然而,该部门没有提供有关该患者的详细信息,FDOH 发言人表示其他信息是保密的。疾病预防控制中心建议采取预防措施,例如使用驱蚊剂、穿防护服和清除积水。

What to know about dengue fever as Florida reports 1st death and cases rise - ABC13 Houston

Original source text (English)
24/7 LiveHoustonSouthwestSoutheastNorthwestNortheast 49° Health & Fitness What to know about dengue fever as Florida reports 1st death and cases rise Florida has confirmed 152 locally acquired cases of dengue this year. Watch ABC News live news stream and get 24/7 latest, breaking news coverage, and live video. ByMary Kekatos Tuesday, September 22, 2026 Share Follow ABC13 on Google The Florida Department of Health confirmed the state's first death from dengue fever this year as cases of the mosquito-borne illness continue to rise. The department did not provide any details about the patient including name, age, sex or residence. The Tampa Bay Times, which first reported the death, described the patient as an 80-year-old Tampa woman who died on Sept. 4. This 2006 photo provided by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention shows a female Aedes aegypti mosquito in the process of acquiring a blood meal from a human host. James Gathany/Centers for Disease Control and Prevention via AP, File "The Department has completed its epidemiological investigation and acknowledges the first dengue fever-related death in Hillsborough County. To protect the privacy of the individual, additional details and information regarding the case are confidential," a spokesperson for the FDOH told ABC News in a state,emt/ The FDOH did not immediately return ABC News' request for comment. For the week ending Sept. 12, Florida has confirmed 152 locally acquired cases of dengue, according to data from the state health department. This is the highest number recorded since 2023, when the state saw 189 locally acquired cases over the course of the year, data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention shows. Hillsborough County -- which includes Tampa -- has reported the most cases so far this year with 134, according to the FDOH. Other counties with confirmed cases include Miami-Dade, Orange, Palm Beach, Pasco and Pinellas. Dengue viruses are spread through bites from infected Aedes species mosquitoes, mostly found in tropical and subtropical regions, according to the CDC. These mosquitoes are also responsible for spreading Zika and chikungunya viruses. Top Stories The seasonal chill is here with morning temps in the 30s through Friday 3 hours ago 18-wheeler driver dead after fuel tanker overturns, catches fire near North Loop, officials say Trespassing juveniles find dead man in abandoned downtown Houston building, HPD says Dengue can be caused by dengue virus strains 1, 2, 3 or 4. A person also can be infected with dengue multiple times over the course of their life. About one in four people who contract dengue develop symptoms which typically last two to seven days and are usually mild. This may include fever, rash, nausea, vomiting, muscle pain, joint pain and bone pain. About one in 20 infected people will develop severe dengue, which can lead to shock, internal bleeding and, in some cases, death, the CDC said. There are no disease-specific treatments for dengue. The CDC recommends rest, fluids and over-the-counter medications to relieve symptoms. Patients with severe illness often need to be hospitalized and receive additional supportive treatments, such as intravenous fluids. The CDC warns patients not to take aspirin or ibuprofen; it can trigger inflammation or bleeding of the stomach lining. Currently, there is no dengue vaccine available in the U.S. In 2021, the U.S. Food and Drug Administration approved a vaccine for children between ages 9 and 16 who have a laboratory-confirmed previous dengue virus infection and live in areas where dengue is endemic, but it has since been discontinued. Methods to help prevent dengue infection include using effective mosquito repellents, wearing long-sleeve shirts and long pants when outside and staying in places with air conditioning when possible. The CDC also recommends emptying containers of still or stagnant water to eliminate places where mosquitos can breed. ABC News' Youri Benadjaoud contributed to this report. Dengue Is No Longer Dhaka’s Problem Alone - https://dailyasianage.com/

News Brief
Dengue cases are increasing in Sylhet division, Bangladesh, previously considered safe from the disease. This year, 144,371 cases and 452 deaths have been reported in Bangladesh, with Sylhet recording 6 deaths and 72 patients in hospital. The risk of local transmission is increasing, with children under ten having the highest death rate. The division is increasing surveillance and funding for mosquito control, but lacks a permanent workforce. Authorities are treating every case as possibly local and triggering larval surveys.
Original source text (English)

The patients now reaching our hospital suggest the division's luck is running out. Dengue is a viral infection passed from person to person by the female Aedes aegypti mosquito. The virus comes in four types, known as serotypes, and recovering from one does not protect against the others. A second infection with a different type is what most often turns an ordinary fever into severe dengue, which can cause bleeding, shock and death. Discover more Online news portal E-Paper access Newspaper advertising space That science matters this year. Bangladesh is fighting two outbreaks at once: measles, linked to close to 1,000 deaths since March, and a dengue season that refuses to end. In the 24 hours to 8am on 5 November, seven more people died of dengue and 1,596 were admitted to hospital, taking this year's toll to 452 deaths and 144,371 cases, according to the Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS). October, with 174 deaths, was the deadliest month of 2026. The country has already passed last year's totals, and all four serotypes are now circulating. For two decades, dengue was treated as a Dhaka problem. That picture no longer holds, and Sylhet, long seen as the country's safest division, may be next to learn it. On 28 October, the Sylhet City Corporation's administrator said every dengue patient identified in the city so far had reportedly been infected outside it. I am not so sure. I began ward practice as a student nurse at North East Medical College and Hospital, Sylhet, on 28 July 2025. In my first week we had a single dengue patient, and our first question to anyone with a high fever was: have you been to Dhaka recently? Fifteen months later, I checked the vital signs of children with fever, rash, body pain and vomiting, and more and more of them have not been to Dhaka at all. That alone does not prove local transmission. But it is a signal to investigate, not to explain away. Dengue has already outgrown Dhaka Reviewing 22 years of outbreaks, Hossain and colleagues found them growing larger and more frequent, reaching even the Rohingya camps in Cox's Bazar in 2022. A systematic review by Sharif et al. counted 565,438 cases and 2,587 deaths between 2000 and early 2024, with DENV-3 dominant (57%) and August the peak month. In the 2023 outbreak, 52% of cases came from outside Dhaka. Children under ten had the highest death rate. In Tangail, then a non-endemic district, Islam et al. studied 123 children, mostly from rural homes. All had fever, 42% had low platelets and 26.8% developed haemorrhagic fever or shock. Why Sylhet has been spared so far Dhaka has long been the epicentre, accounting for more than half of all cases and deaths in past outbreaks. In 2023, it recorded 193,216 cases and 980 deaths, while Sylhet had 1,435 cases and one death. In 2025, Dhaka had more than 32,000 cases; Sylhet had 426 and two deaths. This year, DGHS data up to 4 November show 50,292 admissions and 183 deaths in Dhaka division alone. Ogieuhi et al. name five drivers: climate, urbanisation, density, insecticide resistance and low awareness; only 52.7% of people surveyed knew that clean, stagnant water breeds mosquitoes. Dhaka, with more than 20,000 people per square kilometre, endless construction and choked drains, is ideal for Aedes aegypti. Sylhet has a fraction of that density and, historically, fewer Aedes mosquitoes. Discover more Get Health Tips Newspaper subscription Digital newspaper access Rain helps too. Sylhet's divisional health director, Mahbubul Alam, told The Daily Star that around 5,500mm of annual rainfall keeps water moving, calling rain a blessing for Sylhet. Research on the 2023 outbreak agrees: wetter divisions reported fewer cases. The warning signs are already here Sylhet MAG Osmani Medical College Hospital opened a dengue corner in June. On 3 August, a 54-year-old woman died there, the division's first dengue death this year. Habiganj, with its factories and close links to Dhaka, has recorded the most cases in the division. As of early November, Sylhet has six deaths and 72 patients in hospital. Low numbers also hide a weakness. A national serosurvey found past dengue exposure of about 88% in urban Chattogram but just 3% in rural Moulvibazar. Few people here have ever met the virus, so an outbreak could spread fast, and every first infection sets up a riskier second one. What the ward teaches My job as a second-year B.Sc. nursing student is to check vital signs, monitor platelets and watch for warning signs of dehydration, electrolyte imbalance and hypovolaemic shock. We give paracetamol for fever and pain, never NSAIDs such as ibuprofen, which raise bleeding risk, and avoid intramuscular injections when platelets are low. Patients need plenty of fluids, and the wrong painkiller bought from a pharmacy can turn a mild case dangerous. Families should know that the most dangerous phase often comes as the fever falls: persistent vomiting, severe abdominal pain, bleeding gums or unusual drowsiness mean going to hospital immediately. What Sylhet must do now Sylhet's advantage is borrowed from the weather. Keeping it needs four things. A permanent mosquito-control workforce. The city corporation has none and hires daily-wage workers by the season. Its chief health officer says each of 42 wards needs four to five workers; last December, about 45 worked daily across the whole city, and spraying happened two or three times a year instead of monthly. Discover more Bangladesh news coverage English language news Read Newspapers Treat every case as possibly local. A patient with no travel history should trigger a larval survey around their home and workplace. Its new ward-by-ward drive should follow where patients live. Fix the drains. Sylhet's waterlogging project has been delayed repeatedly, with costs up 44%. Every blocked drain undoes the work of the rain. Avoid Dhaka's mistakes. Dhaka's two city corporations spent about Tk 707 crore over nine years without shielding residents. Sylhet should fund source reduction and year-round surveillance, not just fogging. Let the water flow Let water flow; do not let it stay. Clear drains, empty flower tubs, cover water tanks and dispose of anything that holds rain, from tyres to bottle caps, every week, because a new generation of mosquitoes can emerge within about a week of rain. Sleep under a net. Sylhet still has flowing water and early cleaning drives. But if it loses its grip, a population with so little immunity could be hit harder and faster than anyone expects. Rain has been Sylhet's blessing. It cannot be our strategy. Vagyasree Nandi is a B.Sc. Nursing student at North East Nursing College, Sylhet. Location and population
Bangladesh, Sylhet division.
Event
Dengue cases are increasing in Sylhet division, previously considered safe from the disease. The number of dengue cases and deaths has surpassed previous years, with a significant number of cases not linked to travel to Dhaka.
Scale, impact and risk
This year, 144,371 dengue cases and 452 deaths have been reported in Bangladesh, with Sylhet division recording 6 deaths and 72 patients in hospital. The risk of local transmission is increasing, with children under ten having the highest death rate.
Response, status and uncertainty
The division is increasing surveillance and funding for mosquito control, but lacks a permanent workforce. The risk of local transmission is under investigation, with calls to treat every case as possibly local and trigger larval surveys.

研究人员估计过去 35 年全球登革热病例增加了 34 倍 - CIDRAP

Researchers estimate 34-fold rise in global dengue cases in past 35 years - CIDRAP
新闻简报Researchers have estimated a 34-fold increase in global dengue cases from 1990 to 2024, highlighting the disease's growing importance. The study, presented at the Annual Meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene, analyzed data from 143 countries and used statistical methods to fill gaps in surveillance records. It found that 74 million cases were reported worldwide during the period, with 10.7 million unaccounted for in WHO databases. The true scale of infections is believed to be even higher, possibly reaching 390 million annually, as many cases are mild or asymptomatic. The Florida dengue outbreak in 2026, which included 1,071 reported cases and one death, exemplifies the disease's spread into new regions. The study underscores the need for better surveillance and control strategies, especially as dengue becomes more synchronized globally. However, the findings are based on reported data and do not include specific details on current response measures.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究估计,从 1990 年到 2024 年,全球登革热病例增加了 34 倍,最近一次佛罗里达州 暴发 发生在 2026 年。发布日期为 2026 年 11 月 24 日 (N3)。
地点与对象
该研究涵盖全球数据,特别提到美国佛罗里达州以及该州 2026 年报告的 1,071 例病例 (N12)。
事件
研究人员根据扩展的 监测 数据和统计方法估计,过去 35 年全球登革热病例增加了 34 倍(N3、N8)。
规模、影响与风险
The study estimates 74 million global dengue cases during the period, with 10.7 million unreported. The authors note that the true scale is likely much higher, possibly 390 million infections annually (N6, N9).
应对、状态与不确定性
研究人员的建模框架旨在改善登革热的预测和控制,但该研究基于报告的数据,并未提供当前应对措施的具体细节(N13)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Researchers estimate 34-fold rise in global dengue cases in past 35 years - CIDRAP

News Brief
Researchers have estimated a 34-fold increase in global dengue cases from 1990 to 2024, highlighting the disease's growing importance. The study, presented at the Annual Meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene, analyzed data from 143 countries and used statistical methods to fill gaps in surveillance records. It found that 74 million cases were reported worldwide during the period, with 10.7 million unaccounted for in WHO databases. The true scale of infections is believed to be even higher, possibly reaching 390 million annually, as many cases are mild or asymptomatic. The Florida dengue outbreak in 2026, which included 1,071 reported cases and one death, exemplifies the disease's spread into new regions. The study underscores the need for better surveillance and control strategies, especially as dengue becomes more synchronized globally. However, the findings are based on reported data and do not include specific details on current response measures.

Topics & Projects Antimicrobial Stewardship Chronic Wasting Disease Osterholm Update Podcasts Newsletters About CIDRAP CIDRAP in the News Our Staff Our Director Osterholm in the Press Shop Merchandise Contact Us About Search Support Menu Researchers estimate 34-fold rise in global dengue cases in past 35 years News brief November 24, 2026 Mary Van Beusekom, MS Topics Dengue Share NIAID This year’s Florida dengue outbreak is a strong example of the mosquito-borne disease’s encroachment into new regions, along with a 34-fold increase in global incidence from 1990 to 2024, according to research presented this week at the Annual Meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene (ASTMH) in Maryland. A team led by researchers at the London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine expanded existing surveillance data from 143 countries and used statistical methods to fill gaps in country-level data and convert annual totals into monthly counts. “Dengue is an emerging infectious disease of growing global importance, yet the pace of its expansion remains poorly quantified, because surveillance data are inconsistently reported across countries and over time,” they wrote. True worldwide burden likely much higher The investigators estimated that, during the study period, cases tallied 74 million worldwide, including 10.7 million infections not recorded in World Health Organization databases. Outbreaks have doubled in cyclical strength and become increasingly synchronised globally. “Reported incidence increased 34-fold, with growth fastest in recently emerging regions in Africa, the Middle East and South Asia,” the authors said in the study abstract. “Outbreaks have doubled in cyclical strength and become increasingly synchronised globally.” But the true global scale is believed to be far greater—perhaps as many as 390 million infections annually—because three of four infections are mild or don’t cause symptoms and because case counts represent only those reported by health authorities and surveillance systems. Common symptoms include aches and pains, nausea, and rash; severe cases require hospitalization. There is no treatment for dengue other than supportive care, but deaths are rare. For example, of the 1,071 cases reported in Florida this year, one person died. The researchers’ modeling framework offers a generalizable method of reconstructing incomplete surveillance data and “offers a foundation for improved prediction and control of dengue during a period of declining incidence for most other infectious diseases,” the study authors concluded. Quick takes: Advance access to Ebola vaccine, chlorhexidine cloth shortage, more screwworm in Texas News brief November 24, 2026 Stephanie Soucheray, MA Topics Ebola Misc Emerging Topics New World Screwworm Today Gavi, the Vaccine Alliance, announced an advance purchase commitment to secure access to up to 1.5 million doses of ChAdOx1 BDBV, a Bundibugyo-strain Ebola vaccine candidate developed jointly by Serum Institute of India and the Oxford Vaccine Group at the University of Oxford. “The Ebola outbreak continues to devastate communities in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, we must act with urgency to ensure the regulatory pathways are in place to quickly roll out this vaccine candidate if the clinical trials show positive results. This is critical to saving lives and keeping the world safe,” said Sania Nishtar, DSc, CEO of Gavi, in a press release. ChAdOx1 BDBV is one of two novel Bundibugyo vaccine candidates that are being tested in the current outbreak, the world’s second-largest in history. The agreement helps ensure doses are available pending necessary regulatory approvals. The Society for Healthcare Epidemiology of America (SHEA) and the Association for Professionals in Infection Control and Epidemiology (APIC) are calling for coordinated action to respond to a significant shortage of 2% chlorhexidine gluconate cloths, which hospitals use to remove pathogens from skin, and are a key tool in preventing surgical-site infections. “This shortage is a reminder that infection prevention interventions depend on a resilient supply chain,” said SHEA President Lisa Maragakis, MD, MPH, in a press release. “Transparency from manufacturers and federal partners about the reasons behind the shortage, alternative supplies, and timelines to restore production of this critical infection prevention product will help healthcare epidemiologists plan and mitigate the negative impact of this disruption.” The US Department of Agriculture added 20 more New World screwworm (NWS) cases to its dashboard, for a new total of 165 cases detected in the United States since June. The most recent detections were in Texas counties, including Zapata, Hidalgo, Starr, and Webb, where cattle and swine have suffered infestations of the parasitic fly. November has been the most active month for NWS detections, with 78, compared with 34 in October. 佛罗里达州报告新增 18 例本地感染登革热病例,总数达到 669 例 - 暴发 今日新闻

Florida reports 18 new locally acquired dengue cases, brings total to 669 - Outbreak News Today
新闻简报2026 年 11 月 25 日,《暴发 今日新闻》报道,佛罗里达州确认了 18 例新的本地感染 登革热 病例,使总数达到 669 例。该报告于同一天发布,重点介绍了该州正在发生的登革热 暴发 病例。佛罗里达州卫生部负责确认,但没有提供有关调查、测试或应对措施的进一步细节。没有具体说明受影响的人群,但病例被描述为本地感染。证据中没有提及具体的死亡或风险水平。本报告重点关注登革病毒,它是主题合约下的主要目标。该信息基于佛罗里达州卫生部的调查结果,文章没有提及任何相关实体或硬性排除。
查看新闻五要素
时间
佛罗里达州卫生部报告称,截至 2026 年 11 月 21 日的一周内,新增 18 例本地感染 登革热 病例。报告发布日期为 2026 年 11 月 25 日。
地点与对象
该报告涉及佛罗里达州,特别提到了当地发生的登革热病例。没有具体说明受影响的人群,但据报告病例为本地感染。
事件
佛罗里达州报告新增 18 例本地感染登革热病例,使总数达到 669 例。佛罗里达州卫生部证实了这一消息。
规模、影响与风险
报告指出新增 18 例病例,使总数达到 669 例。证据中没有提及具体的死亡人数或风险水平。
应对、状态与不确定性
报道称,佛罗里达州卫生部已确认这些病例。证据中没有提供有关调查、测试或应对措施的详细信息。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Florida reports 18 new locally acquired dengue cases, brings total to 669 - Outbreak News Today

News Brief
On November 25, 2026, Outbreak News Today reported that Florida had confirmed 18 new locally acquired dengue fever cases, raising the total to 669. The report, published on the same date, highlights the ongoing dengue outbreak in the state. The Florida Department of Health is responsible for the confirmation, but no further details about the investigation, testing, or response measures are provided. The affected population is not specified, but the cases are described as locally acquired. No specific deaths or risk levels are mentioned in the evidence. This report focuses on dengue virus, which is a primary target under the topic contract. The information is based on the Florida Department of Health's findings, and the article does not mention any related entities or hard exclusions.

佛罗里达州的登革热 暴发 反映了全球趋势 - 《科学星期五》

Florida’s dengue outbreak mirrors a global trend - Science Friday
新闻简报《科学星期五》杂志于 2026 年 11 月 20 日发表的这篇文章讨论了佛罗里达州的登革热 暴发,这不是一个孤立事件,而是全球趋势的一部分。报告指出,登革热正在激增并蔓延到新的地区,这可能是受到气候变化的影响。这篇文章没有提供具体病例或死亡人数,但强调了 暴发 的更广泛含义。这篇文章对登革热不断增加的 患病率 进行了评论,强调了其与环境因素的联系。证据中没有包含有关公共卫生应对措施的详细信息,例如检测或接触者追踪。内容是完整的原始报告,但缺乏有关 暴发 规模和影响的具体数据。

Florida’s dengue outbreak mirrors a global trend - Science Friday

News Brief
The article from Science Friday, published on November 20, 2026, discusses a dengue outbreak in Florida that is not an isolated incident but part of a global trend. It notes that dengue is surging and spreading to new areas, likely influenced by climate change. The piece does not provide specific case or death counts but emphasizes the broader implications of the outbreak. The article serves as a commentary on the increasing prevalence of dengue, highlighting its connection to environmental factors. No detailed information on public health responses, such as testing or contact tracing, is included in the evidence. The content is a full original report but lacks specific data on the outbreak's scale and impact.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

登革热:持续存在的国家威胁,需要全年采取行动

Dengue: A persistent national threat demanding year-round action
新闻简报登革热仍然是斯里兰卡持续存在的公共卫生威胁,需要全年采取行动。截至 2026 年 11 月,该国记录了 110,431 例登革热病例和 82 例死亡,凸显了该疾病对个人、家庭和国民经济的持续影响。西部省份受到的影响尤为严重,但登革热已在所有地区蔓延。在直接威胁消退后,公众的注意力往往会减弱,导致重复出现 暴发。预防工作必须延伸到 暴发 期间之后,解决导致蚊子繁殖的环境因素。2026 年 8 月的国家登革热审查强调需要采取循证干预措施、加强部门间协调以及持续的社区参与。监测 数据和当地趋势应指导采取主动措施,以确定高风险地区并防止疾病升级。登革热不再局限于城市中心,如果没有支持性的公共系统,仅靠个人责任是不够的。

2026 年 11 月 26 日,星期四 关于我们 广告 联系方式 FT TV 电子纸菜单 � 版块 网络主页 专栏作家 专栏作家 Ajantha Dharmasiri Ajith de Alwis D.B.S.

斯里兰卡,重点关注西部省和全国社区。
事件
登革热仍然是斯里兰卡持续存在的公共卫生威胁,尽管在 监测 和控制方面做出了努力,但在 2026 年已有病例和死亡报告,且 监测 的反复出现。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年 11 月,斯里兰卡记录了 110,431 例登革热病例,82 人死亡。该疾病给个人、家庭、医疗机构和国民经济带来沉重负担。即使在一个拥有丰富临床管理经验的国家,它也是一种潜在的致命疾病。
应对、状态与不确定性
预防登革热必须成为国家的共同责任。敦促地方当局确保有效的固体废物管理、排水系统维护和环境卫生。2026 年 8 月的国家登革热审查强调了基于证据的干预措施、更强的协调、持续的准备和积极的社区参与。监测数据、蚊子繁殖指数、天气信息和当地流行病学趋势应在监测升级之前指导有针对性的干预措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue: A persistent national threat demanding year-round action

News Brief
Dengue remains a persistent public health threat in Sri Lanka, demanding year-round action. As of November 2026, the country recorded 110,431 dengue cases and 82 deaths, highlighting the disease's ongoing impact on individuals, families, and the national economy. The Western Province has been disproportionately affected, but dengue spreads across all regions. Public attention often wanes after immediate threats subside, leading to repeated outbreaks. Prevention efforts must extend beyond outbreak periods, addressing environmental factors that allow mosquito breeding. The National Dengue Review in August 2026 emphasized the need for evidence-based interventions, stronger coordination among sectors, and sustained community involvement. Surveillance data and local trends should guide proactive measures to identify high-risk areas and prevent disease escalation. Dengue is no longer confined to urban centers, and individual responsibility alone is insufficient without supportive public systems.

Thursday Nov 26, 2026 ABOUT US ADVERTISING CONTACT FT TV E-PAPER MENU � Sections Network Home Columnists Columnists Ajantha Dharmasiri Ajith de Alwis D.B.S. AI SUMMARY GENERATED ONLY FROM THIS ARTICLE’S TEXT. Prevention cannot be seasonal when the threat is constant Dengue has become an all-too-familiar part of life in Sri Lanka. Each year, particularly with the arrival of the monsoon rains, rising case numbers prompt renewed public concern, mosquito-control campaigns and calls for greater vigilance. Yet, as the immediate threat subsides, public attention often diminishes, only to return with the next outbreak. This recurring pattern raises an important question: Have we begun to accept dengue as an inevitable part of life, rather than a preventable public health threat that demands sustained national action? For decades, dengue has remained one of Sri Lanka’s most persistent public health challenges. Despite considerable progress in disease surveillance, clinical management and vector control, it continues to impose a substantial burden on individuals, families, healthcare institutions and the national economy. The magnitude of this challenge is evident in our epidemiological experience. In 2017, Sri Lanka experienced its largest recorded dengue epidemic, with 186,101 suspected cases and 440 reported deaths. Nearly a decade later, the country continues to face significant outbreaks, demonstrating that dengue remains far from being brought under sustainable control. The situation in 2026 is particularly concerning. According to surveillance figures reported in November, Sri Lanka had recorded 110,431 dengue cases and 82 deaths by November 2026. The Western Province continued to bear a disproportionate burden, while transmission remained a concern across the country. These figures represent much more than epidemiological statistics. Behind every reported case is an individual whose health, education or livelihood may be disrupted, a family experiencing anxiety and, in severe cases, a patient requiring intensive medical care. Every preventable death is a reminder that dengue remains a potentially fatal disease, even in a country with extensive experience in its clinical management. Early recognition Early recognition and timely medical intervention are therefore critical. Dengue commonly presents with fever, headache, pain behind the eyes, muscle and joint pain, nausea and sometimes a rash. However, the most dangerous period may begin as the fever subsides, when some patients enter the critical phase of illness. Warning signs such as severe abdominal pain, persistent vomiting, mucosal bleeding, marked lethargy, restlessness or signs of circulatory compromise require urgent medical assessment. Patients with suspected dengue should seek timely medical advice, maintain appropriate hydration and avoid medications such as aspirin and ibuprofen because of the risk of bleeding. Early identification, careful monitoring and appropriate fluid management can substantially reduce the risk of death. Importantly, dengue is no longer a concern confined to Colombo or other densely populated urban centres. Although the Western Province accounts for a substantial proportion of cases, the disease affects communities across Sri Lanka. Rapid urbanisation, inadequate waste management, construction activities, population movement, rainfall patterns and environmental conditions all contribute to the persistence and spread of mosquito breeding sites. Changing weather patterns and increasing climatic variability further complicate dengue prevention. However, rainfall alone does not explain the problem. Poor environmental management, neglected premises, improperly stored water and inadequate community participation create conditions that allow mosquito populations to thrive. Fundamental challenge The fundamental challenge is therefore not simply controlling dengue during an outbreak but preventing the conditions that allow repeated outbreaks to occur. Sri Lanka has developed considerable expertise in dengue management, supported by dedicated healthcare professionals, public health teams and national surveillance systems. The National Dengue Control Unit and other health authorities have played an important role in coordinating prevention and response activities. Nevertheless, the continuing burden of disease demonstrates that healthcare-sector interventions alone cannot provide a lasting solution. Dengue prevention must become a shared national responsibility. Every household should routinely inspect its surroundings and eliminate potential mosquito breeding sites. Water-collecting containers, discarded tyres, blocked gutters, flowerpots, construction materials and improperly managed waste require particular attention. Water-storage containers must be securely covered and regularly cleaned. Personal protection through appropriate clothing, mosquito repellents and measures to prevent mosquito bites during the day should also be encouraged. However, individual responsibility, while essential, is insufficient without supportive public systems. Local authorities must ensure effective solid-waste management, drainage maintenance and environmental sanitation. Schools and workplaces should adopt regular, structured dengue-prevention programmes rather than limiting activities to periods of heightened transmission. Construction sites abandoned buildings and public spaces require systematic monitoring and accountability. Equally important is strengthening coordination between the health sector, local government, education authorities, environmental agencies, community organisations and the private sector. Dengue control should be embedded within routine institutional responsibilities, supported by clear accountability, reliable surveillance and timely action. Sri Lanka’s National Dengue Review in August 2026 reinforced the importance of evidence-based interventions, stronger operational coordination, sustained preparedness and active community participation. These priorities should now translate into measurable action at national, provincial, district and community levels. Proactive approach A more proactive approach is essential. Surveillance data, mosquito breeding indices, weather information and local epidemiological trends should be used to identify high-risk areas and guide targeted interventions before outbreaks escalate. Public communication must also move beyond occasional warnings towards sustained education that encourages practical behavioural change. Ultimately, successful dengue control should not be measured solely by the number of emergency campaigns conducted or premises inspected during an outbreak. It should be judged by sustained reductions in transmission, preventable hospitalisations and deaths, supported by healthier and safer environments. Sri Lanka has demonstrated its capacity to achieve remarkable public health outcomes through strong preventive services, community engagement and coordinated national programmes. The same commitment must now be directed towards dengue. The goal should not merely be to respond effectively to the next dengue outbreak, but to prevent that outbreak from occurring wherever possible. Dengue does not respect administrative boundaries, socioeconomic divisions or seasonal public health priorities. Our response must therefore be continuous, coordinated and inclusive. The responsibility extends beyond the Ministry of Health. It belongs to every institution, every community and every citizen. Dengue may be a year-round threat but preventing it must become a year-round national commitment. (The author is Consultant Paediatrician, President-Elect, Sri Lanka College of Paediatricians and Immediate Past President, Sri Lanka Medical Association) FT Alert In 2017, Sri Lanka experienced its largest recorded dengue epidemic, with 186,101 suspected cases and 440 reported deaths. Nearly a decade later, the country continues to face significant outbreaks, demonstrating that dengue remains far from being brought under sustainable control. The situation in 2026 is particularly concerning. According to surveillance figures reported in November, Sri Lanka had recorded 110,431 dengue cases and 82 deaths by November 2026 Recent columns

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

岘港主动控制不断增加的登革热 暴发。- 越南.vn

Da Nang proactively controls the increasing dengue fever outbreak. - Vietnam.vn
新闻简报越南岘港市正在积极应对不断增加的登革热 暴发病例,11月16日至23日报告病例2,219例,较前一周增加15%。暴发 集中在几个病区,包括 Ngu Hanh Son(1,363 例)和 An Khe(817 例)。卫生部门正在利用数字流行病学地图来识别高风险地区,并与地方当局和居民协调以加强预防工作。积极措施包括在高风险地区喷洒化学物质、开展公众宣传活动以及加强监测检测和控制蚊子繁殖。卫生部已成立工作组来评估情况并确保医疗设施做好应对大量患者涌入的准备。尽管做出了这些努力,但由于公众意识较低以及有利于蚊子繁殖的天气条件,持续传播的风险仍然很高。应对措施正在进行中,重点是环境卫生和社区参与。
查看新闻五要素
时间
岘港市的登革热 暴发一直在增加,最新统计时间为11月16日至23日。该消息发布日期为2026年11月25日,活动正在进行中,并正在采取积极措施。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在岘港市,特别是在 Ngu Hanh Son、An Khe、Son Tra、Thanh Khe、Dien Ban Dong 等地区。受影响的人群包括这些病房的居民以及岘港更广泛的社区。
事件
岘港市正在积极控制不断增加的登革热 暴发。卫生部门正在加强监测,应用数字流行病学地图,并建议公民预防这种疾病。暴发 已得到确认,各病区报告了具体病例数。
规模、影响与风险
11月16日至23日,岘港市共报告登革热病例2219例,较前一周增加15%。像Ngu Hanh Son这样的特殊病房有1,363例,An Khe有817例。由于意识低下和环境因素,持续传播的风险很高。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生部门正在通过宣传、环境卫生、监测等方面加大力度。CDC 开发了数字流行病学地图系统,并进行了 554 项主动 媒介 调查。应对措施正在进行中,正在采取措施解决高风险地区并准备医疗设施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Da Nang proactively controls the increasing dengue fever outbreak. - Vietnam.vn

News Brief
Da Nang city, Vietnam, is actively managing an increasing dengue fever outbreak, with 2,219 cases reported from November 16th to 23rd, marking a 15% rise compared to the previous week. The outbreak is concentrated in several wards, including Ngu Hanh Son (1,363 cases) and An Khe (817 cases). The health sector is employing digital epidemiological maps to identify high-risk areas and coordinate with local authorities and residents to enhance prevention efforts. Proactive measures include chemical spraying in high-risk zones, public awareness campaigns, and intensified surveillance to detect and control mosquito breeding. The Department of Health has established working groups to assess the situation and ensure medical facilities are prepared to handle an influx of patients. Despite these efforts, the risk of continued spread remains high due to low public awareness and favorable weather conditions for mosquito breeding. The response is ongoing, with a focus on environmental sanitation and community engagement.
Original source text (English)
English Login Homepage Topic News Political System Destination Event Travel Happy Vietnam Enterprise Product Heritage Museum Figure Multimedia Data Home Địa phương Đà Nẵng Da Nang proactively controls the increasing dengue fever outbreak. DNO - The number of dengue fever cases in Da Nang city continues to increase. Given the complex situation, the health sector is strengthening surveillance, applying digital epidemiological maps to identify risk areas, and advising every citizen to proactively prevent the disease to stop its spread. Báo Đà Nẵng•25/11/2026 Follow Vietnam.vn on Google Add Vietnam.vn on Google 0 Chemical spraying in Ngu Hanh Son ward is being carried out in high-risk areas to limit the spread of the disease. Photo: L.D. Many localities are recording a surge in the number of cases. According to health statistics, from November 16th to 23rd, the city recorded 2,219 cases of dengue fever, a 15% increase compared to the previous week. The number of cases increased in many localities such as the wards of Ngu Hanh Son, An Khe, Son Tra, Thanh Khe, Dien Ban Dong and some other areas. In Ngu Hanh Son ward, Ms. Dang Thi Ha, a representative of the ward's health station, said that the locality has recorded 1,363 cases of dengue fever so far. In response to this situation, the health station, in coordination with 54 residential groups, intensified propaganda efforts through the public address system, mobile outreach, and direct visits to every alley, street, and lane to encourage residents to clean up the environment and remove unnecessary water containers to prevent mosquito breeding. According to Ms. Ha, dengue fever prevention and control requires regular coordination between the health sector, local authorities, and the people. If only cases are dealt with when they appear without maintaining environmental hygiene and eliminating mosquito larvae in each household, the risk of the disease continuing to spread remains very high. Information on dengue fever prevention and control is disseminated to each residential area through the public address system. Photo: L.D. In An Khe ward, 817 cases of dengue fever have been recorded. Ms. Nguyen Thi Thuy, Deputy Director of the ward's Health Station, said that one of the current difficulties is the low level of awareness of disease prevention among some residents. Despite regular local awareness campaigns and environmental sanitation efforts, there is still a tendency towards complacency, with a lack of consistent inspection and removal of objects that collect stagnant water around homes. The An Khe Ward Health Station continues to coordinate with neighborhood groups to reach out to each household, especially in areas with a high number of cases, guiding people to identify mosquito breeding grounds and proactively eliminate mosquito larvae and pupae. Strengthen propaganda and mobilize people to proactively clean the environment and eliminate mosquito larvae and pupae. Photo: L.D. In Dien Ban Dong ward, the unpredictable weather with alternating sunshine and rain creates favorable conditions for mosquitoes to breed and develop. Mr. Tran Loc Quang, Director of the ward's Health Station, said that dengue fever is an acute infectious disease caused by the Dengue virus, transmitted through Aedes mosquitoes. The disease typically begins with symptoms such as a sudden high fever, headache, muscle and joint pain, pain behind the eyes, fatigue, loss of appetite, and possibly a rash and nausea. Because the initial symptoms are quite similar to some common illnesses, patients often underestimate the condition and self-treat at home. Facing the risk of an increase in disease outbreaks, the Dien Ban Dong Ward Health Station, in coordination with 27 residential groups, is stepping up propaganda and mobilization efforts to encourage residents to regularly inspect their living areas and address stagnant water spots after rain. You may also like Locals participate in training sessions to learn about measures to prevent and control dengue fever. Photo: L.Đ. Applying digital epidemiological maps to identify risk areas. Dr. Nguyen Dai Vinh, Director of the City's Center for Disease Control (CDC), said that the unit has developed a digital epidemiological map system for online surveillance to improve the effectiveness of monitoring, detecting, and handling high-risk areas. The system allows for the visualization of epidemiological data on maps, tracking the distribution of cases by commune and ward; it also integrates functions for displaying case locations, heat maps, delineating buffer zones for disease control within a 200m radius, analyzing overlapping areas between outbreaks within a 400m radius, and monitoring the progression of the epidemic over time. This provides the health sector with an additional tool to identify high-risk areas, supporting localities in determining priority areas for monitoring and treatment. Alongside the application of technology, the CDC, in coordination with commune and ward health stations and medical facilities, is strengthening regular surveillance to detect cases and outbreaks early; investigating disease vectors to assess mosquito density and larval indices to guide appropriate treatment measures. According to CDC monitoring data, the unit conducted 554 proactive vector surveillance surveys, recorded 85 high-index sites, and organized 165 monitoring sessions on mosquito larva eradication in key areas. These results are used to classify areas into three risk groups, serving as a basis for advising local authorities on organizing disease prevention and control: high-risk areas require mosquito larva and pupa eradication once a week; areas with high mosquito, larva, and pupa indices require eradication every two weeks; and remaining areas maintain eradication once a month. The health sector recommends that people spend 10 to 15 minutes each day checking for and removing stagnant water sources and unnecessary water containers to prevent mosquito breeding grounds. People are pouring water and cleaning household items to prevent the breeding of mosquito larvae. Photo: L.D. Intake, treatment, and disease prevention efforts start from each family. According to Dr. Truong Van Trinh, Deputy Director of the City Health Department, dengue fever is increasing in the area due to several reasons, including the disease's cyclical outbreaks occurring every 3-8 years. Following the outbreak in 2022, the number of cases has shown signs of increasing again. In addition, the erratic weather with alternating rain and hot sunshine creates favorable conditions for mosquitoes to breed, making disease prevention and control efforts more difficult. In response to this situation, the Department of Health established three working groups, comprising department leaders and the city's CDC, to conduct surveys and assessments in various localities in order to promptly direct response measures. The health sector is also inspecting preparations at medical facilities, requiring proactive measures regarding human resources, hospital beds, equipment, and medicines to ensure the reception, admission, and treatment of patients. Healthcare facilities such as Da Nang Hospital, Da Nang Obstetrics and Pediatrics Hospital, Son Tra Regional Health Center, and other units are required to review their treatment capacity and be ready to respond when the number of patients increases. Training on patient classification, diagnosis, treatment, and referral is also required. Recently, Da Nang Obstetrics and Pediatrics Hospital organized a training course on the diagnosis and treatment of dengue fever in children under 6 years old; Da Nang Hospital also provided professional training for medical staff throughout the city on the diagnosis, treatment, and referral of adult patients. The Department of Health directed the CDC to procure additional chemicals and proactively support localities that are lacking or have not yet been able to purchase them, so as not to interrupt the implementation process. Dr. Truong Van Trinh advises that localities need to strengthen environmental sanitation, eliminate mosquito larvae and pupae, and treat stagnant water spots, especially after rain. Each household should proactively check and remove discarded bottles, discarded items, plant pots, and tires that can collect water; tightly cover containers holding household water and regularly change the water in flower vases. People also need to proactively get vaccinated against dengue fever, wear long-sleeved clothing, sleep under mosquito nets, and cooperate with health authorities when spraying chemicals to kill mosquitoes.
