巴西研究发现转基因蚊子可减少 69% 的登革热和基孔肯雅热感染 - 印度时报

Brazil study finds 69% fewer dengue, chikungunya infections with modified mosquitoes - The Times of India
新闻简报在巴西贝洛奥里藏特进行的一项为期四年的研究发现,释放携带沃尔巴克氏菌的改良蚊子可显着减少 5,700 多名儿童的登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率,减少近 69%。这项研究在美国热带医学和卫生学会会议上发表,比较了有和没有转基因蚊子的社区之间的感染率。尽管该策略显示出希望,但由于案例不足,研究人员无法可靠地得出其对抗寨卡病毒的有效性的结论。世界蚊子计划一直在全球范围内推广这种方法,研究结果支持将其用作传统蚊子控制方法的替代方法。研究还指出,美国佛罗里达州在 2026 年经历了大规模登革热 暴发,当地感染人数超过 650 例,凸显了此类干预措施的持续相关性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究在巴西进行了四年,并于 2026 年 11 月 21 日报告了结果。该战略的有效性在 2024 年巴西 重症登革热 暴发 期间得到了认可。
地点与对象
这项研究在巴西贝洛奥里藏特进行,涉及 5,700 多名儿童。它还提到了美国佛罗里达州,那里发生了大规模登革热 暴发,截至 2026 年 11 月,当地感染人数已超过 650 例。
事件
巴西的一项研究发现,在释放携带沃尔巴克氏体细菌的改良蚊子的地区,登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率降低了 69%。该研究涉及 5,700 多名儿童,历时四年。研究人员还对寨卡病毒感染进行了检测,但由于病例数量不足,无法得出可靠的结论。
规模、影响与风险
该研究报告称,在携带沃尔巴克氏体的蚊子地区,儿童的登革热和基孔肯雅热感染率下降了近 69%。它还指出,截至 2026 年 11 月,佛罗里达州已有超过 650 例本地感染登革热感染病例,其中四分之三病例发生在希尔斯伯勒县。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是在美国热带医学和卫生学会的一次会议上提出的。世界蚊子计划一直在努力推广这一方法。这些发现被认为是强有力的证据,但由于案例有限,无法可靠地确定针对寨卡病毒的有效性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Brazil study finds 69% fewer dengue, chikungunya infections with modified mosquitoes - The Times of India

News Brief
A study conducted in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, over four years found that releasing modified mosquitoes carrying Wolbachia bacteria significantly reduced dengue and chikungunya infections by nearly 69% among over 5,700 children. The research, presented at the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene meeting, compared infection rates between neighborhoods with and without the modified mosquitoes. While the strategy showed promise, researchers could not reliably conclude its effectiveness against Zika due to insufficient cases. The World Mosquito Program has been promoting this approach globally, and the findings support its use as an alternative to traditional mosquito control methods. The study also noted that Florida, USA, experienced a large dengue outbreak in 2026, with over 650 locally acquired infections, highlighting the ongoing relevance of such interventions.
Original source text (English)
Edition IN IN US UK GCC English English हिन्दी मराठी ಕನ್ನಡ தமிழ் বাংলা മലയാളം తెలుగు ગુજરાતી Weather Sign In TOI Science Nature Archaeology Wildlife Earth Space Discovery Today's ePaper Live News Science News Brazil study finds 69% fewer dengue, chikungunya infections with modified mosquitoes Brazil study finds 69% fewer dengue, chikungunya infections with modified mosquitoes Edited by Vijaya Srivastava / TIMESOFINDIA.COM / Nov 21, 2026, 21:38 IST Save Comments Share Aa +Text Size Small Medium Large Print File photo of a mosquito feeling on hand (AP Photo/Rick Bowmer, File) Scientists have reported strong new evidence that specially bred mosquitoes carrying a naturally occurring bacteria can significantly reduce the spread of dengue and chikungunya, after a major study involving more than 5,700 children in Brazil found a nearly 69 per cent drop in infections in areas where the mosquitoes were released.The four-year study, conducted in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, found that children living in neighbourhoods with these mosquitoes were far less likely to contract the two mosquito-borne viruses than those in similar areas without them. The protection remained effective even during Brazil's severe dengue outbreak in 2024, researchers said on Saturday.The findings were presented at a meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene. They offer fresh support for using Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes as an alternative to relying only on pesticides and measures to prevent mosquito bites.“This is really exciting,” said Dr David Hamer, a global health expert at Boston University who was not involved in the study.He said combining the release of Wolbachia mosquitoes with existing mosquito-control measures could “knock dengue down to much lower levels” in many places. by Taboolaby Taboola Sponsored LinksSponsored Links Promoted LinksPromoted Links You May Like Undo The findings come as Florida faces an unusually large dengue outbreak, which appears to be the biggest recorded in any US state since at least the 1940s.Dengue cases in the continental US are usually linked to international travel. However, Florida health officials had recorded more than 650 locally acquired infections as of Friday. About three-quarters of these cases were reported in Hillsborough County, which includes Tampa. How the mosquito strategy works The approach involves breeding mosquitoes with Wolbachia, a type of bacteria that occurs naturally in many insects. The bacteria are harmless to humans but are not commonly found in Aedes aegypti mosquitoes, which spread dengue.Wolbachia significantly reduces the mosquitoes' ability to transmit certain viruses. Female mosquitoes also pass the bacteria on to their offspring.Scientists release both male and female mosquitoes carrying the bacteria into a targeted area. As they breed with the local mosquito population, Wolbachia gradually becomes established. This reduces the ability of the wider mosquito population to spread viruses.The nonprofit World Mosquito Program has been leading efforts to develop and expand this approach outside the US. Earlier field trials in Indonesia and other locations have also shown significant reductions in dengue cases. What the Brazil study found The latest research went beyond tracking reported infections. Researchers tested children's blood for evidence of dengue, chikungunya and Zika infections. This helped identify infections that may have gone undiagnosed because people experienced only mild symptoms.The study followed more than 5,700 children over four years. All participating neighbourhoods received the same pesticide treatments and other mosquito-control measures. They then compared infection rates between children living in the two groups of neighbourhoods.The results showed about a 69 per cent reduction in dengue and chikungunya infections among children in areas where the specially bred mosquitoes were released.Researchers could not establish whether the strategy reduced Zika infections because there were too few cases to draw a reliable conclusion. View five news elements
Time
The study was conducted over four years in Brazil, with findings reported on November 21, 2026. The effectiveness of the strategy was noted during Brazil's severe dengue outbreak in 2024.
Location and population
The study took place in Belo Horizonte, Brazil, involving over 5,700 children. It also referenced Florida, USA, where a large dengue outbreak occurred, with more than 650 locally acquired infections as of November 2026.
Event
A Brazilian study found a 69% reduction in dengue and chikungunya infections in areas where modified mosquitoes carrying Wolbachia bacteria were released. The study involved over 5,700 children and was conducted over four years. Researchers also tested for Zika infections but could not draw reliable conclusions due to insufficient cases.
Scale, impact and risk
The study reported a nearly 69% drop in dengue and chikungunya infections among children in areas with Wolbachia-carrying mosquitoes. It also noted that Florida had over 650 locally acquired dengue infections as of November 2026, with three-quarters of these cases in Hillsborough County.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study was presented at a meeting of the American Society of Tropical Medicine and Hygiene. The World Mosquito Program has been leading efforts to expand this approach. The findings are considered strong evidence, but the effectiveness against Zika could not be reliably established due to limited cases.
